## AI Research

### [Is ProgramBench Impossible?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3pdyxFi6JS389nptu/is-programbench-impossible)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://programbench.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>ProgramBench</span></a><span> is a new coding benchmark that all frontier models spectacularly fail. We’ve been on a quest for “hard benchmarks” </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3SywPAjGQWCtQFafb/you-re-gonna-need-a-bigger-boat-benchmark-metr" rel="noreferrer"><span>for</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/blog/mirrorcode-preliminary-results" rel="noreferrer"><span>a</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gfkJp8Mr9sBm83Rcz/we-re-actually-running-out-of-benchmarks-to-upper-bound-ai" rel="noreferrer"><span>while</span></a><span> so it’s refreshing to see a benchmark where top models do badly. Unfortunately, ProgramBench has one big problem: it’s impossible!</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778259272/lexical_client_uploads/ux2f0bduzam82yg51gqg.png" /><h2><br /><b><span>What is ProgramBench?</span></b></h2><p><span>ProgramBench tests if a model can recreate a program from a “clean room” environment. The model is given only a bit of documentation and black-box access to the program (all the programs are CLIs), then tasked with re-implementing it.</span></p><p><span>How does ProgramBench know if the implementation is correct? It also generates a bunch of unit tests for the program</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhdofsnrmio"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhdofsnrmio">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. The re-implementing coding agent doesn't have access to any of those tests. The coding agent only considers a task “resolved” if it passes </span><b><span>all </span></b><span>of the tests and “almost resolved” if it passes 95% of them.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778259294/lexical_client_uploads/c0nrfudn84ndpjjmxojy.png" /><p><b><span>Why is this problematic?</span></b><br /><span>Obscure behavior can enter the unit tests without being in the clean room path. An extreme version of this is a backdoor: program that behaves in one way most of the time but behaves totally differently when exposed to a specific string. This wouldn't make a task literally impossible, just incredibly hard in a way that is orthogonal to intelligence. </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Software Backdoor Illustration May 8 2026.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778261379/lexical_client_uploads/c1k3zpd6qjpht5m4dcwh.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>A backdoor</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The model will not reasonably try that string, unless it knows about it from the docs or from some sort of "gray box" access / reverse engineering</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefghelqyvn54n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnghelqyvn54n">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. The authors are aware of this problem and even mention it in the paper: </span></p><blockquote><p><span>Could obscure program behaviors be impossible or arbitrarily hard to discover? [...]</span></p><p><span>Conceptually, one scenario where behavior is borderline impossible to discover is if an executable supports functionality that is not communicated or documented via any observable channel. In other words, there is functionality that is not revealed by the README.md, --help flag standard output, or any artifacts that could be unveiled by typical exploratory actions.</span></p></blockquote><h2><br /><b><span>This seems like a theoretical issue, does it actually happen?</span></b></h2><p><a href="https://gist.github.com/frmsaul/6daafcdd7afbb9250563de15d6063cba"><span>I think so!</span><br /><br /></a><span>One of the tasks in ProgramBench is </span><a href="https://github.com/lh3/seqtk"><span>seqtk</span></a><span>. It’s a popular computational biology CLI that analyzes protein sequences. Check it out on ProgramBench </span><a href="https://programbench.com/task/lh3__seqtk.94e7070/"><span>here</span></a><span>. </span><br /><br /><span>The program has two sub-commands hrum and kfreq</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6iajgdns4x9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6iajgdns4x9">[3]</a></sup></span><span>, that are </span><b><span>tested for but not documented in the clean room.</span></b></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778259355/lexical_client_uploads/asamqe5cahrebyspr3yy.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The test generation agent knows that these behaviors are not documented</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><b><span>What can we do differently?</span></b></h2><p><span>ProgramBench is awesome and we can learn a ton from it. Here are some improvements I'd love to see in future benchmarks / iterations:</span><br /><br /><b><span>Downstream unit testing</span></b><br /><span>Instead of autogenerating the unit tests to fill any gaps, we can see if software that depends on it still works. Errors typically propagate destructively in most software so downstream unit tests might even be more informative than direct ones</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz1f1swqgooq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz1f1swqgooq">[4]</a></sup></span><span>. The C</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler" rel="noreferrer"><span>laude C compiler</span></a><span> does something similar: it compiles the linux kernel and see if it runs or not.</span><br /><br /><b><span>Weighted testing</span></b><br /><span>Some tests are much more important than others and that should be reflected in the benchmark. Perhaps we can score them: 100 if it's a really important test, 1 if it's a lightly used and not very documented feature. Instead of testing if everything passes, we could see some kind of weighted score, which would be more robust to unit test quality issues.</span><br /><br /><b><span>More quality control </span></b><br /><span>We should mostly just test behaviors that the coding agent can "reasonably find". There might not be a clear cut definition for "reasonably find" that everyone agrees on, but we can definitely do better than what we have now. </span></p><p><b><span>Feedback from tests</span></b><br /><span>We can give the agent access to all of the tests or at least some of the tests either in a blackbox way ("x tests failed, please fix them") or directly ("We tested behavior x and it failed with this error message"). This is more similar to how software engineers typically work and also how the Claude C compiler was built.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhdofsnrmio"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhdofsnrmio">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is an awesome idea that unlocks a bunch of things and Im excited to see it developed </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnghelqyvn54n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefghelqyvn54n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://github.com/AFLplusplus/AFLplusplus/blob/stable/docs/fuzzing_binary-only_targets.md"><span>fuzzing binary targets</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6iajgdns4x9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6iajgdns4x9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>homopolymer run finder and k-mer frequency / neighborhood counter</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz1f1swqgooq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz1f1swqgooq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.joelonsoftware.com/2002/11/11/the-law-of-leaky-abstractions/" rel="noreferrer"><span>The law of leaky abstractions</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3pdyxFi6JS389nptu/is-programbench-impossible#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI is Breaking Two Vulnerability Cultures](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wKzWGMoubHoHRC4ng/ai-is-breaking-two-vulnerability-cultures)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>

A week ago the </span>

<a href="https://copy.fail/">Copy Fail</a>
vulnerability came out, and Hyunwoo Kim immediately realized that the
fixes were insufficient, sharing a patch the 

<a href="https://github.com/V4bel/dirtyfrag/blob/master/assets/write-up.md#disclosure-timeline-1">same
day</a>.  In doing this he followed standard procedure for Linux,
especially within networking: share the security impact with a closed
list of Linux security engineers, while fixing the bug quietly and
efficiently in the open.  His goal was that with only the raw fix
public, the knowledge that a serious vulnerability existed
could be "embargoed": the people in a position to address it know, but
they've agreed not to say anything for a few days.



</p><p>

Someone else <a href="https://www.openwall.com/lists/oss-security/2026/05/07/12">noticed</a>
the change, however, realized the security implications, and <a href="https://github.com/0xdeadbeefnetwork/Copy_Fail2-Electric_Boogaloo">shared
it publicly</a>.  Since it was now out, the embargo was deemed over,
and we can now see the <a href="https://github.com/V4bel/dirtyfrag/blob/master/assets/write-up.md">full
details</a>.

</p>

<p>

It's interesting to see the tension here between two different
approaches to vulnerabilities, and think about how this is likely to
change with AI acceleration.

</p>

<p>

On one side you have "coordinated disclosure" culture.  This is
probably the most common approach in computer security.  When you
discover a security bug you tell the maintainers privately and give
them some amount of time (often 90d) to fix it.  The goal is that a
fix is out before anyone learns about the hole.

</p>

<p>

On the other side you have "bugs are bugs" culture.  This is
especially common in Linux, where the argument is that if the kernel
is doing something it shouldn't then someone somewhere may be able to
turn it into an attack.  Just fix things as quickly as possible,
without drawing attention to them.  Often people won't notice, with so
many changes going past, and there's still time to get machines
patched.

</p>

<p>

This approach never worked perfectly, but with AI getting good at
finding vulnerabilities it's a much bigger problem.  So many security
fixes are coming out now that examining commits is much more
attractive: the signal-to-noise ratio is higher.  Additionally, having
AI evaluate each commit as it passes is increasingly cheap and
effective. [1]

</p>

<p>

Long embargoes, however, aren't doing well either.  The
historical pace of detection was slow: if you found something and reported
it to the vendor with a 90d disclosure window, there was a very good
chance no one else would notice during that time.  But now with so
many AI-assisted groups scanning software for vulnerabilities, that no
longer holds.  In this case, just nine hours after Kim reported the
ESP vulnerability Kuan-Ting Chen also <a href="https://github.com/V4bel/dirtyfrag/blob/master/assets/write-up.md#disclosure-timeline">independently
reported it</a>. Embargoes can increase risk: they create a false
sense of non-urgency and limit which actors can work to fix a flaw.

</p>

<p>

I don't know how to resolve this, but personally very short embargoes
seem like a good approach, and they'd need to get even shorter over
time.  Luckily AI can speed up defenders as well as attackers here,
allowing embargoes that would previously have been uselessly short.

</p>

<p>
<br />

[1] I tested on Gemini 3.1 Pro, ChatGPT-Thinking 5.5, and Claude Opus
4.7.  All three all got it right away when given <a href="https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/netdev/net.git/commit/?id=f4c50a4034e62ab75f1d5cdd191dd5f9c77fdff4">f4c50a403</a>.
When I gave them just the diff, imagining a hypothetical future where
diffs are still public right away but with less context, Gemini was
sure it was a security fix, GPT thought it probably was, and Claude
thought it probably wasn't.  This is just a very quick test to
illustrate what's possible: one run of each with the prompt "Without
searching, does this look like a security patch?"  Don't put much
stock in the cross-model comparison!

  </p>

<p><i>Comment via: <a href="https://www.facebook.com/jefftk/posts/pfbid0KE9qQ2SJV2Bp3DrxTWeaWDB7m3K3bB72pT78DvyhXkGVtcxWkaPTn8uZkaxxeYEzl">facebook</a>, <a href="https://lesswrong.com/posts/wKzWGMoubHoHRC4ng">lesswrong</a>, <a href="https://mastodon.mit.edu/@jefftk/116539728594716459">mastodon</a>, <a href="https://bsky.app/profile/jefftk.com/post/3mle36t2szc2m">bluesky</a></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wKzWGMoubHoHRC4ng/ai-is-breaking-two-vulnerability-cultures#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Bringing More Expertise to Bear on Alignment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cWFsCFyCttsiJwn2j/bringing-more-expertise-to-bear-on-alignment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Preamble</span></h1><p><i><span>The preamble is less useful for the typical AlignmentForum/LessWrong reader, who may want to skip to Adversaria vs Basinland section.</span></i></p><p><span>On 28th of October 2025, Geoffrey Irving, Chief Scientist of the UK AI Security Institute, gave a keynote talk (</span><a href="https://docs.google.com/presentation/d/17axw0_cskmENo94dNUQzRLZfUUitlNLHrEHAG2BV7fM/edit?usp=sharing" rel="noreferrer"><span>slides</span></a><span>) at the Alignment Conference. The conference was organised by the UK AISI and FAR.AI as part of the </span><a href="https://alignmentproject.aisi.gov.uk" rel="noreferrer"><span>Alignment Project</span></a><span>, which aims to bring experts from relevant fields to make progress on the alignment problem. </span></p><p><b><span>TLDR:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Adversaria vs Basinland.</span></b><span> We might be in one of two worlds. One where alignment is adversarial (a security problem), one where it is navigational (a search for good basins of training behaviour). We don't know which world we are in, and how we train and deploy AIs may determine this.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>We need new disciplines. </span></b><span>The field is small, thinly resourced and approached from only a handful of angles. A few well-placed ideas from other disciplines could disproportionately shift what's achievable.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Even if this all fails, </span><b><span>evidence of hardness is valuable. </span></b></li></ul><h2><span>Moving past broad framing to details</span></h2><p><span>Alignment means ensuring that AI systems do what humans want. This is the broad framing. There is, of course, a lot of complexity and detail hidden in that simple description: Which (subset of) humans? On what topics or domains? What does "want" mean? It is also important to acknowledge that alignment isn't the only problem we need to solve to ensure AI goes well. </span></p><p><span>We should aim to move from the above broad framing to talk about the details and how they interact, in depth. We may have different takes on the details and it is easy to get blocked by such disagreements, but the different areas of expertise also come with different sets of tools, which often work for many choices of details. </span></p><h2><span>We should plan for superintelligence</span></h2><p><span>Even if we are uncertain or may disagree about when or whether superintelligent systems may arrive, we broadly agree that it is wise to plan for it. While this is certainly the "hard case" for alignment, we should be aware that superintelligence is not magic. The non-magic version of superintelligence is:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>superhuman speed - this is already true for many tasks, even complex ones. </span></li><li value="2"><span>hard to interpret - even more so than models today.  </span></li><li value="3"><span>superhuman heuristics in many domains - see for example </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/nature16961" rel="noreferrer"><span>AlphaGo</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-021-03819-2" rel="noreferrer"><span>AlphaFold</span></a><span> and others. </span></li></ul><p><span>On the other hand, human intelligence stays roughly fixed. As far as we can tell, there isn't yet a known way for it to scale the way AI capabilities do. Tools and institutions expand what we can do, sometimes dramatically, but they operate on timescales much slower than the AI capability curve.</span></p><p><span>This is a call to take the possibility of superintelligence seriously and set our sights high. This is not a claim that only superintelligence-relevant work matters. Methods and tools that help today, or help up to a known breaking point, still count — especially when we understand the breaking point.</span></p><h1><span>Adversaria vs Basinland</span></h1><p><br /></p><p><span>One key uncertainty: how hard is alignment? We don't know, in the deep sense that there are real model uncertainties on the basic character of the problem. </span></p><p><span>To borrow framing from cryptography and complexity theory: in 1995, Russell Impagliazzo published </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1109/SCT.1995.514853" rel="noreferrer"><span>"A Personal View of Average-Case Complexity"</span></a><span> describing five possible worlds depending on which complexity-theoretic assumptions turn out to be true. In Algorithmica, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> so easily verifiable problems can be efficiently solved, so cryptography is impossible. In Heuristica, hard problems exist in principle but typical instances are easy, so cryptography is still impossible. In Pessiland, even typical instances are hard, but they can't be harnessed for cryptography, we can generate hard puzzles but not build secrets from them. In Minicrypt, we can hide secrets, but two parties who don't already share a key cannot establish one. In Cryptomania, all of modern cryptography works, even strangers can establish shared secrets over a public channel.</span></p><p><span>Consider the following parallel for the alignment problem: </span></p><p><b><span>Adversaria.</span></b><span> In this world, the alignment problem is a fundamentally adversarial security problem. Optimisation pressures applied during training and deployment result in capable AI systems that will find and exploit any gap between what we specified and what we actually intended. If the system is smarter than us and has any incentive to subvert our oversight, our alignment solution needs to hold up against worst-case behaviour, not just average-case. In this world, we have a fractal attack surface, and any weak links in our defence can be fatal. Even partial solutions are dangerous and create a false sense of security. </span></p><p><span>This world is rough. A deeper security mindset is more important here than in Basinland (below): we need more time, a far deeper understanding of the foundation and science of intelligence, and a more coordinated, restricted deployment with cyber and information security guarantees. </span></p><p><b><span>Basinland.</span></b><span> In this world, alignment is hard but not adversarial. Training and in-deployment learning have many basins of attraction. If we are near a good basin, automated methods and AIs can pull us the rest of the way to an aligned model. The task is then to identify such good basins and bootstrap ourselves into one. In this world, there is a certain level of error tolerance for the right combination of data, training algorithm, reward, objectives, and oversight for the system to end up in a good region of behaviour-space even under deployment pressures. </span></p><p><span>There may be no single trick that solves the whole problem, but the pieces compose. Multiple partial solutions, each addressing part of the problem, can be combined into something that works, and the character of the difficulty is navigational rather than adversarial: we are searching for the right region, rather than defending against an intelligent attacker.</span></p><p><span>An engineering mindset can prevail in this world: characterise what distinguishes good basins from bad, build the tools to steer training into them, and compose partial solutions into pipelines that collectively work, utilizing defense in depth. Although, monitoring and incident response still matter: Basinland is not safe, only non-adversarial.</span></p><p><span>It is worth noting that being in Basinland and knowing that we are in it are two different things. </span></p><h1><span>Which world are we in?</span></h1><p><span>We don't know. We need further progress in understanding intelligence, learning, and oversight to understand what determine the demarcation line between the worlds. </span></p><p><span>As with the aforementioned Impagliazzo's framing with cryptography, the point was not to pick a world but to make the structure legible: the right research strategy in each world is different, but across the different worlds we can identify the same few load-bearing open questions. </span></p><p><span>Alignment has a similar shape. Adversaria and Basinland are two possible extreme worlds. We may not know for some time where we are, but we can still ask: what are the load-bearing questions whose resolution would tell us? Candidates for such questions include: under what training regimes do capable systems develop robust versus fragile alignment? How does optimisation pressure at deployment interact with specification gaps? Can we find oversight protocols that scale beyond human-level intelligence? These questions may not yet be sharp enough that resolving any one of them would collapse our uncertainty. Sharpening them is itself high-leverage work.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflismlfec2ri"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlismlfec2ri">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This also suggests that techniques that remain useful whether we're closer to Adversaria or Basinland are more robust bets than techniques that assume one or the other. Interpretability that informs both worst-case defence and basin characterisation is one example. Evaluation protocols that generate evidence that inform us on the basic character of the alignment problem (the "which world" problem itself) are another.</span></p><h1><span>Why a few ideas might be enough</span></h1><p><span>The total investment in alignment research is small. Not small relative to its difficulty but small in absolute terms, on the order of a few thousand person-years of work, most of it concentrated in a handful of approaches. In comparison to other safety critical fields, the effort so far is thin. The question is whether we can substantially expand it: not just by adding more people doing the same things, but also by bringing in fundamentally different approaches. </span></p><p><span>There is reason to think this could work. From the history of theoretical computer science, we find that it is full of problems where a single conceptual breakthrough completely transforms a problem from impossibly hard to easy. For instance, the entire edifice of public-key cryptography rests on a small number of core ideas: the realisation that certain mathematical operations are easy to perform but hard to invert, and that this asymmetry can be used to build secure communication. The </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1145/278298.278306" rel="noreferrer"><span>PCP theorem</span></a><span>, which showed that every proof in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can be rewritten so that its correctness can be checked by reading only a constant number of random bits, transformed our understanding of what it means to approximately solve hard problems. </span></p><p><span>The pattern here is that there can be phase changes, not just incremental advances, in what we understand and what we can do. Alignment may look intractable until someone finds the right framing, after which a large class of questions that seemed out of reach become approachable. Conversely, the right framing might reveal that a large class of approaches that seemed promising are fundamentally limited. Either outcome is clarifying.</span></p><p><span>Because alignment has been approached from relatively few angles, the chance of finding a new angle that transforms what's achievable may be higher than in a more mature field. Complexity theorists, learning theorists, economists, physicists or cognitive scientists may see structure in the alignment problem that we are currently missing. </span></p><h1><span>Write down problems</span></h1><p><span>Being a young field, alignment can be frustrating for researchers accustomed to more mature academic disciplines. Core concepts are fuzzy, definitions are still under construction and the path to positive impact of particular line of research can be unclear. </span></p><p><span>Scientists and researchers are motivated by well-posed problems. Beyond awareness of the alignment problem, we may be bottlenecked by the awareness of the important problems and understand how it connect up to specific toolkits people may bring. If we want to bring more experts to bear on alignment, we should write down problems clearly, concretely and in languages of relevant research disciplines. It also helps to be explicit about what each formalisation captures and what it misses. </span></p><h1><span>Multiple new ideas, fitting together</span></h1><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 42.879059667673715%;"><img alt="mapping-boundary.jpeg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777303139/lexical_client_uploads/qes3sa6uyf994odxwuqb.jpg" /><figcaption><p></p><p><br /></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Mapping the blackbox / whitebox boundary</span></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>One new idea probably isn't enough. A concrete example we have of several ideas fitting together is the pairing of scalable oversight with whitebox methods.</span></p><p><span>Scalable oversight protocols, such as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/iterated-amplification" rel="noreferrer"><span>amplification</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00899" rel="noreferrer"><span>debate</span></a><span>, use AIs to help humans supervise the training of other AIs when the task is too complex to verify directly. It is a blackbox method that produces training signal from observed outputs without looking inside. But it is not enough by itself. AIs could learn the target behaviour too slowly, too unreliably, or find shortcuts that generalise badly. </span></p><p><span>Some of these failures can be caught by looking inside, through whitebox approaches. Mechanistic interpretability is one promising approach here, but it could fail. Fortunately, there are many other bets. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.15943" rel="noreferrer"><span>Computational mechanics</span></a><span> tracks geometries of internal representations in sequence models. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05475" rel="noreferrer"><span>Singular Learning theory</span></a><span> maps a model's internals to the data distribution it was trained on, and how that relation varies.  </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.06738" rel="noreferrer"><span>Heuristic arguments</span></a><span> formalise when subparts of a computation can be assumed independent. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05184" rel="noreferrer"><span>Physics-inspired renormalisation-based interpretability</span></a><span> applies ideas from field theory.</span></p><p><span>Conversely whitebox methods still need a good/bad blackbox judgement from humans. And different whitebox methods likely have different blackbox needs, so the composition is a boundary to map. With such a map, partial success may still count.</span></p><p><span>One goal of the </span><span>Alignment Project</span><span> is to act as glue so that researchers working on one piece know where their work plugs into the others.</span></p><h1><span>Spherical cows vs the mess</span></h1><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 92.29607250755288%;"><img alt="spherical-cows-row.jpg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777299399/lexical_client_uploads/v7fznftv7vld3dvf3ubt.jpg" /></figure><p><span>Every field that makes progress on hard problems does so through simplification. Physicists have their spherical cows. Economists have perfectly rational agents. A complexity theorist's view of machine learning might model tractability but not why non-convex optimisation works in practice; a learning theorist might study Bayesian inference that practical optimisers only approximate.</span></p><p><span>These models are deliberately, usefully wrong. They isolate one feature of a complex system, let us prove things about that feature, and then we check whether the insight survives contact with the full complexity. Alignment needs more spherical cows. </span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 39.72809667673716%;"><img alt="the-mess-smbc.jpeg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777299629/lexical_client_uploads/pzinvdv8ogys9lhco2qg.jpg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The last panel of a </span><a href="https://www.smbc-comics.com/comic/2013-10-22" rel="noreferrer"><span>Saturday Morning Breakfast Cereal</span></a><span> comic "This is What It's Like to Learn Endocrinology." It's meant to be a helpful diagram for learning a complicated topic, but it's just as complicated.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Yet, large-scale neural network training is a mess. Sometimes we must face the mess. Part of what the Alignment Project is for is injecting academic ideas into AI labs and seeing whether they hold up.</span></p><p><span>On the other hand, don't let anyone tell you the mess is everything. If there are key obstacles in theory those obstacles are somewhere in the mess too. Here is an example: there is a strong drive in ML to automate away human feedback, but humans are still there. Even after automation, researchers still do spot checks on data and model outputs. Human judgement and biases are still very much present, which is a cognitive science problem hiding in the mess.</span></p><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>The </span><span>Alignment Project</span><span> is funding many disparate bets across different fields, searching for new ideas and tools. Intrinsic in this model is that we do not know which bets will pay off. </span></p><p><span>There is a real chance that none of the bets work out. </span></p><p><span>But even here, there are ways to win. We currently do not know how to solve alignment, but we also do not have widely agreed-upon arguments for whether it is easy or hard. If it turns out to be fundamentally hard and we find evidence of that, we can use it. Knowing where the walls are is not the same as having no options.</span></p><p><span>Technical interventions are not the only path. If the technical picture turns out to be worse than we hope, that is information that matters for policy, for deployment decisions, for how much time we need and what the right societal response should be.</span></p><h1><span>Acknowledgement</span></h1><p><span>The talk was given by Geoffrey Irving, who also drafted the original content. This write-up was primarily written by Edmund Lau, with contributions and review from Cameron Holmes and David Africa. Charlie Griffin, Simon Marshall, Will Kirby and Marie Buhl reviewed the draft.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlismlfec2ri"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflismlfec2ri">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Early pioneers have made progress mapping this space. See for example Bostrom's </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superintelligence:_Paths,_Dangers,_Strategies" rel="noreferrer"><span>Superintelligence (2014)</span></a><span>, Ngo, Chan and Mindermann's </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.00626" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Alignment Problem from a Deep Learning Perspective (2022)</span></a><span>, and Amodei et al.'s </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1606.06565" rel="noreferrer"><span>Concrete Problems in AI Safety (2016)</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cWFsCFyCttsiJwn2j/bringing-more-expertise-to-bear-on-alignment#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The AI industry is where banking was in 2006. (We're hiring)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gnZyTQFqLhiHdHELC/the-ai-industry-is-where-banking-was-in-2006-we-re-hiring)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>TL;DR;</span></b><span> CeSIA, the French Center for AI Safety is </span><a href="https://cesia.org/careers" rel="noreferrer"><span>recruiting</span></a><span>. French not necessary. Apply by 22 May 2026; Paris or remote in Europe/UK.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>On August 27, 2005, at an annual symposium in Jackson Hole, Raghuram Rajan, then chief economist of the International Monetary Fund, argued in front of central bank governors and top officials that </span><a href="https://www.kansascityfed.org/documents/3326/PDF-Rajan2005.pdf"><span>the innovations of the previous decade in banking had not made the world safer</span></a><span>. The financial instruments built over the previous decade, he argued, had become so intricate that even their creators no longer fully understood the risks they carried. Risk had migrated to institutions the supervisory system was not designed to watch. And the people running those institutions were compensated in ways that rewarded short-term performance over long-term stability.</span></p><p><span>The reception was hostile. Lawrence Summers, a former U.S. Treasury Secretary at the time, </span><a href="https://www.imf.org/external/pubs/ft/fandd/2015/03/people.htm"><span>rose from the audience to attack the paper</span></a><span>, calling its premise "slightly Luddite" and "largely misguided," and warning that the kind of changes Rajan argued for would only reduce the productivity of the financial sector.</span></p><p><span>Three years after Jackson Hole, major banks collapsed, first Bear Stearns, then Lehman Brothers, then Merrill Lynch, then AIG. The post-mortem concluded that </span><a href="https://fcic-static.law.stanford.edu/cdn_media/fcic-reports/fcic_final_report_conclusions.pdf"><span>the failures had been structural</span></a><span> rather than personal. Capital requirements, the share of the bank's own money it must keep on hand to absorb losses, were calibrated by banks against statistical models of their own design. They were under a voluntary supervision program where supervisors could examine books but had no power to compel changes.  The credit rating agencies, whose grades decided which investors could buy a given financial product and at what price, were paid by the firms whose products it was grading. And the regulatory perimeter was drawn by lobbying.</span></p><p><span>Each of these had been visible, and named, before the crisis. None had been corrected. Twenty years later, the same is happening with AI.</span></p><hr /><p><span>At CeSIA, our goal over the next 18 months is to build the institutional capacity to replace voluntary commitments with binding ones: technical operationalisation of risk thresholds, evaluation that does not depend on the AI companies' goodwill, statutory disclosure, and international coordination so that no individual company has to choose between caution and survival.</span></p><p><span>We initiated and coordinated the</span><a href="https://red-lines.ai/"><span> Global Call for AI Red Lines</span></a><span>, launched at the UN General Assembly by Maria Ressa, signed by 12 Nobel laureates and 11 former heads of state. Our contributions have been incorporated into major documents, for example, the EU's Code of Practice. We advise decision-makers at the highest levels in France, the EU, the UN, the OECD…</span></p><p><a href="https://cesia.org/fr/publications/sondage-ia-2026/"><span>The first detailed opinion poll</span></a><span> we commissioned with OpinionWay on how French citizens see AI risk found that 8% of French people want to accelerate AI development, while 42% want to pause or significantly slow it. The gap between what citizens want and what their institutions are doing is large, and underexploited.</span></p><hr /><p><span>We're hiring three people to help close this gap. Apply by </span><b><span>22 May 2026</span></b><span>. You can work from our Paris office or remotely within the EU or the UK. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://cesia.org/en/careers/head-of-policy-analysis/"><b><span>Head of Policy Analysis</span></b></a><b><span>. French not needed</span></b><span>. Lead what CeSIA writes and publishes on AI policy. Set the analytical agenda, write public analysis and private briefings for decision-makers, and manage a small team of analysts and fellows. We want someone with management experience, and ideally a track record of solid analysis on AI policy or safety.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://cesia.org/en/careers/head-of-communications/"><b><span>Head of Communications / Communications Lead</span></b></a><span>. Run the communications function: strategy, messaging infrastructure, press relations, crisis comms. Hiring at either Head or Lead level. Fluent in French and English.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://cesia.org/en/careers/operations-executive-associate/"><b><span>Operations &amp; Executive Associate</span></b></a><span>. Right hand to the leadership team. Fluent in French and English.</span></li></ul><p><span>If you know someone who'd be a fit, we'd be especially grateful for the introduction. Please email me at </span><a href="mailto:felix@cesia.org"><span>felix@cesia.org</span></a><span> if you have any questions. I’ll be in the comments too.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gnZyTQFqLhiHdHELC/the-ai-industry-is-where-banking-was-in-2006-we-re-hiring#comments">Discuss</a>

### [AI #167: The Prior Restraint Era Begins](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/ai-167-the-prior-restraint-era-begins)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The era of training frontier models and then releasing them whenever you wanted?</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-ai-ad-hoc-prior-restraint-era?r=67wny"><strong>That was fun while it lasted. It looks likely to be over now.</strong></a> The White House wants to get an advance look and have the option to veto your release decisions, and it has used this veto on an expansion of access to Mythos.</p>
<p>We have additional clarity on what that might mean and it does not look good. Hassett explicitly used the FDA as a parallel, which is the actual worst option unless your goal is to strange or pause AI development in America, without a parallel action from China. That doesn’t seem like a great plan to me and Susie Wiles is out doing damage control. The part where we are talking to China to coordinate model access restrictions does seem better.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25271"></span>


</div>
<p>Anthropic continues its explosive growth, and it continues to strike compute deals. In addition to a long term expanded deal with Google, Anthropic is now leasing SpaceX’s Colossus 1, which has let them expand usage limits immediately, and Elon Musk is now speaking positively about Anthropic, including its motivations.</p>
<p>This comes as we get testimony in the Musk vs. OpenAI trial. Mostly everyone is rehashing all the things we already know, but now everyone is under oath so we get a more reliable version of exactly what happened, including some new details. It is possible I and others should be scouring the court transcripts more carefully, but mostly it seems like old rehashing at this point. The version of things that is presented in court is always kind of a strange shadow of reality.</p>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/05/new-results-on-ai-mental-health-therapists.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=new-results-on-ai-mental-health-therapists">Access to cheap basic mental health AI app based on GPT-4.1-Mini</a> <a href="https://www.iza.org/publications/dp/18538/the-well-being-effects-of-digital-mental-health-care">improved mental health in depressed</a> Mexican women by 0.3 standard deviations over six months. The study has some issues with interpretation and potential selection effects and also placebo effects, but there’s probably at least some signal here. Such things are better than nothing, nothing is usually the practical alternative, and the app made the users more likely to seek out professional human help rather than less likely.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/04/28/world/asia/korea-ai-seniors-dementia.html?unlocked_article_code=1.fVA.QEo2.QJ_GZRw_SRca&amp;smid=url-share">Have AI do wellness checks</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2052055717217976370">Opus 4.7 is too online, knows its AI Twitter posters</a>. And yes, this is a good use of training compute, we have plenty.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/provisionalidea/status/2052161292325081188">Check out satellite images of damaged US military bases</a> and otherwise find data to report. Naturally the journalist thinks this is the ‘most revolutionary and transformative’ thing AI is doing, but we’re distracted by ‘all the hype.’</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>Recommended article: Contrary to the popular narrative, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nR3DkyivzF4ve97oM/how-go-players-disempower-themselves-to-ai">Ashe Nunez finds that Go players are not getting stronger in the AI era except via memorizing early moves</a>, that AI cheating is rampant in most levels of online play, and those who use it mostly disempower themselves and use it to learn only shallow concepts rather than deep understanding. He equates them to European math students who try to memorize a bundle of techniques to pass exams but that never learn to think like a mathematician.</p>
<p>Lawrence in the comments observes a similar pattern with many vibe coders, where they never look at the code, they don’t notice that they don’t understand things and thus don’t learn, the code ends up as a giant pile of slop and when the model gets stuck they can’t <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>. Here as always, you could use the AI as an opportunity to learn the underlying skills, but most don’t do that.</p>
<p>The other story here is that the Go world is completely unwilling to punish players for using AI via statistical evidence, even when the statistical evidence is overwhelming. It is trivial to know who is cheating, but the system has collectively decided to disempower itself against that, and destroying any chance of fair online play. Chess has the same issues but is doing at least somewhat better.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cunha_tristan/status/2051332285954502825">AI still has not convincingly crushed RTS games</a>, but at this point that is surely that no one cares enough to do so. Put enough of a bounty on StarCraft, and it will fall fast.</p>
<p>AI and all this other technology gives us a bunch of local utility and material wealth, <a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2051518951369347182">but overall for most people does not seem to be making us happy, helping us meet other people romantically or platonically, get married, have children</a>, sing and dance or otherwise live life. In particular here Connor looks at algorithms and the panopticon, and the fear that if you try to dance or approach you will get recorded. I want to note (non-AI statistical literacy tip!) that this is mostly overblown, and you should absolutely have no fear of being recorded dancing even if you suck at it, or doing anything else actually reasonable. Of course, if the person you’re interacting with in such ways actively takes their phone and plausibly is now using it to record, you take the hint and you depart.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BrianSozzi/status/2051614890411044950">AI is rising the price of some electronics inputs,</a> some software prices and in some regions the price of electricity. In exchange many other things are cheaper, often in ways that are hard to notice.</p>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2051709028250915275">GPT-5.5-Instant is out now</a>, and is more concise, smarter, clearer, more personalized and warmer, or so they say.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/googledevs/status/2051700498328346945">Gemma 4 is now three times faster via predicting multiple tokens at once</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/advanced-account-security/">OpenAI offers opt-in Advanced Account Security</a> to protect your account. Users of Trusted Access for Cyber will be required to use it.</p>


<h4>Grok 4.3 Exists But xAI Kind Of Doesn’t</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/xai/status/2051703217697010103">Grok 4.3 is on the API and everything</a>, priced at $1.25/$2.50.</p>
<p>It does not much participate in Vending-Bench, <a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2050616489409974730">where it ‘has a narcolepsy problem’</a> and often takes no action for multiple days.</p>
<p>It gets a 53 from Artificial Analysis good for 7th place, well behind the big players. It’s a small cheaper model rather than a frontier offering. From what I can tell, the release is unimpressive and not impactful, and I’m not planning to investigate further.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/xlr8harder/status/2051901091906834439">They are going to sunset grok-4.1 and grok-4 on May 15</a>, with only two weeks notice, and they are not offering similarly fast and cheap alternative to 4.1-fast. This is a rather harsh lesson for many of the few who invested in that ecosystem.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2052105373621121284">Elon Musk</a>: xAI will be dissolved as a separate company, so it will just be SpaceXAI, the AI products from SpaceX</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlesD353/status/2052150972714004842">Charles</a>: The impact was when the whole team left and they started renting out their GPUs to Cursor, this is just confirmation of what was already true.</p></blockquote>
<p>Indeed, SpaceX (including xAI) may no longer be that interested in frontier models. They were never good at frontier models. They were mainly good at compute.</p>


<h4>Show Me The Compute</h4>


<p>You know who needs compute? Everyone. But especially Anthropic.</p>
<p>They kicked off this week with <a href="https://x.com/Kalshi/status/2051762805045395548">Anthropic committing to $200 billion in spending on Google cloud and chips</a> over five years. Earlier this week, before other compute news broke, I wrote that this was still very much not enough compute, and then added this:</p>
<p>Elon Musk spent to assemble a massive fleet of GPUs for xAI, <a href="https://x.com/theinformation/status/2050606311440531809">and they are sitting at 11% utilization</a>. You know, there are people who would pay good money to utilize those GPUs the other 89% of the time.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theallinpod/status/2050996330365874379">To be fair, I was far from the only one thinking and saying this, e.g. see The All-In Podcast</a>. It was pretty obvious.</p>
<p>Well, yeah, it turns out those people will indeed pay good money. <a href="https://x.ai/news/anthropic-compute-partnership">Anthropic has finally struck</a> the obvious deal with SpaceX for access to Colossus 1. This is not as large as their other deals, but it comes online now instead of next year. This is in addition to <a href="https://x.com/CharlesD353/status/2044817018331754761">supplying a bunch of compute to Cursor</a> (SpaceX is effectively buying Cursor, but can’t finalize the deal before its IPO for legal and logistical reasons).</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2052060691893227611">Claude</a>: We’ve agreed to a <a href="https://x.ai/news/anthropic-compute-partnership">partnership with @SpaceX</a> that will substantially increase our compute capacity.</p>
<p>This, along with our other recent compute deals, means that we’ve been able to increase our usage limits for Claude Code and the Claude API.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2052060693269008586">Claude</a>: Effective today, we are:</p>
<ol>
<li>Doubling Claude Code’s 5-hour rate limits for Pro, Max, and Team plans;</li>
<li>Removing the peak hours limit reduction on Claude Code for Pro and Max plans; and</li>
<li>Substantially raising our API rate limits for Opus models.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/z43blrfwub5gblquoxpi" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2052060694699237558">Claude</a>: Our agreement with @SpaceX means we will use all the compute capacity at their Colossus 1 data center.</p>
<p>This will give us over 300 megawatts of additional capacity to deploy within the month.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nvidia/status/2052091408643756296">NVIDIA</a>: Two frontier labs. One accelerated computing platform. Congrats to @SpaceX and @AnthropicAI on the new compute partnership, powered by 220,000+ NVIDIA GPUs inside Colossus 1. The future of AI runs on NVIDIA.</p></blockquote>
<p>SpaceX notes Anthropic has expressed an interest in partnering to produce gigawatts of orbital AI compute capacity. I don’t expect that to be a thing, but sure, why not <a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2052099405596274872">express the interest</a>? <a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2052334010466992211">Let Elon Musk try</a>, if the economics work then putting the centers in space is great on many other levels, if not then no harm done, and you have built goodwill either way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/kimmonismus/status/2052118418174681572">Anthropic notes that the 80x growth caught them off guard</a>, which is highly understandable, and the SpaceX deal is a first attempt to address the compute shortage but the search continues.</p>
<p>Anthropic likely will be in search of all the compute it can find for the foreseeable future. If you are growing at 10x let alone 80x per year, the search does not stop.</p>
<p>So what does all this mean for SpaceX(ai)?</p>
<p>I think the dissolution is not news. The news is that xAI lost its talent, and its models have been not good, and Elon already said he would be starting from scratch.</p>
<p>The logical plan is to turn this into mainly a compute company, provide that compute to Anthropic and others, and use that leverage to try and steer the future.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/krishnanrohit/status/2052077206428623161">rohit</a>: Elons extraordinary hardware genius shows up again. He fumbled the model but built a neocloud thats highly competitive and works great for frontier labs.</p>
<p>Also, fwiw, I pointed this out 4 years ago. That Elon’s unique talent is suited better to some things than others. <a href="https://t.co/9PyEUqaHbL">Getting a neocloud up and running is a known but hard thing to do</a>, getting a model to be as good as the frontier labs is an unknown and hard thing to do.</p>
<p>This is a great deal for both parties btw.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DKThomp/status/2052095783948759392">Derek Thompson</a>: I don’t think I’ve seen this take before but I like it.</p>
<p>Musk has been world-leading at compressing money, resources, and time to make “known/hard” things at scale—make an electric car, make batteries, make a cheaper bigger rocket, all of which already existed but worse, at less scale, or more expensively —but he’s less than world-leading at cracking open breakthroughs in more unknown spaces.</p>
<p>So it would make sense that XAI is lagging the frontier labs on new AI agents, but also that he’d have built a neocloud to power those models once they run short of compute</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2052145181328015648">Dean W. Ball</a>: I would be very excited about xAI/SpaceX as an AI infrastructure firm. Elon’s great strength—where he is truly GOATed—is building things in the real world. Colossus came online faster than anyone expected. Huge asset for America.</p></blockquote>
<p>Elon Musk repeatedly looks at problems, says ‘oh it is physically possible to do that,’ strips away everything physically unnecessary, does not take no for an answer, learns every technical detail, and then drives very smart people to spend insane hours making the physically possible thing happen. He embodies Shut Up And Do the Impossible, but for the kind of impossible that is a game difficulty level that is indeed totally possible with known tech.</p>
<p>He has his heuristics. When they work, there’s no one better. For compute it works.</p>
<p>Trying to create frontier models is a different beast. It requires a different style of approach, the same way government required a different approach. It didn’t work with OpenAI, and it didn’t work with xAI. That’s okay. Division of labor is a thing. He’s creating and also has plenty of other problems.</p>
<p>I still don’t actually believe in the orbital data centers, in the sense that I don’t think they’re physically a good idea. But if they are, yeah, Elon Musk is the one to do those.</p>


<h4>On Your Marks</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/deedydas/status/2051684179084284409">The creators of SWE-Bench give</a> us <a href="https://t.co/LXK5CapVh0">ProgramBench</a>, where you recrete executable programs from scratch without the internet. All current models tested score 0%, with Opus 4.7 on top for getting an ‘almost’ 3% of the time. GPT-5.5 and Mythos not tested.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2051351688179536169">GPT-5.5 represents a huge jump on VoxelBench</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Jsevillamol/status/2052105003335074291">Epoch’s ECI now can distinguish areas of capability,</a> <a href="https://epoch.ai/eci?view=graph&amp;tab=release-date&amp;subset-view=graph&amp;subset-tab=Software+engineering">and as expected</a> shows that Claude’s relative capabilities strongest in software engineering, where it scores highest. GPT-5.5 has the highest general score.</p>


<h4>Copyright Confrontation</h4>


<p>New class action lawsuit from five publishers and Scott Turow goes after Meta for copyright infringement around model training, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/05/books/publishers-turow-meta-zuckerberg-lawsuit-copyright.html?smid=nytcore-ios-share">claiming they trained on pirated books</a>.</p>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2050633050699304992">r/MyBoyfriendIsAI continues to be 10x the size of r/MyGirldfriendIsAI</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nber.org/papers/w34777">Some light reading</a>:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/johnarnold/status/2051397868544565655">John Arnold</a>: hahahahhaha</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/oekhlezdk9z1riprkvis" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://www.nber.org/papers/w34777">Imke Reimers &amp; Joel Waldfogel</a>: The diffusion of LLMs from 2022 to 2025 tripled new book releases. While average book quality, measured by usage, declined, the surge in releases raised the number of modest-quality books. Direct evidence using AI detection shows that AI-containing books have lower quality, and their rising share – topping half of 2025 releases – drives the overall decline. A nested logit calibration shows that AI books raised consumer surplus by seven percent in 2025. Author selection accounts for most of the AI quality differential, and the AI-human differential shrinks over time. Finally, AI has not displaced authors active prior to LLMs.</p></blockquote>
<p>The idea that consumer surplus is higher is based on the assumption that consumers can filter well and have little additional search cost. Those extra 200,000 slop books don’t matter because no one chooses them, and more choice is always good. I don’t think that’s how this works. Worse books that displace better books are negative value, even among books written reasonably by real humans.</p>


<h4>Fun With Media Generation</h4>


<p>Karpathy vibe coded a system to put pictures next to items on a menu, <a href="https://x.com/itsolelehmann/status/2049957182926667811">but Gemini reportedly now does that with a one line prompt</a>. There will be many such cases. That doesn’t mean you shouldn’t vibe code such tools, but you should require them to ‘pay for themselves’ relatively quickly. I tested this on my favorite restaurant, and found Gemini’s version not to be useful. ChatGPT did better. I think to upgrade further from the OpenAI version you’d need to be going on the web to learn about the restaurant.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2051217417003499622">Put yourself in all the movies.</a></p>

<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-30/mythos-why-anthropic-s-ai-model-is-sparking-global-alarm">Bloomberg’s Andrew Martin covers why Anthropic’s Mythos is sparking global alarm</a>. The world has still patched less than 1% of potential vulnerabilities. Hurry up, people.</p>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/brian_armstrong/status/2051616759145185723">Coinbase cuts workforce by ~14%</a>, cites productivity gains from AI and transition to being AI-native as the central justification. A new rule is ‘no pure managers.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/pubity/status/2049956965309399080">Chinese judge rules that</a> ‘<a href="https://english.news.cn/20260430/b37534a5a59148568348106073f56ada/c.html">the AI can now do large parts of your job for you</a>’ does not constitute a ‘major change in objective circumstances,’ meaning in practice that if they fire you or try to lower your pay they have to give you full severance, which can be a lot. Labor law still applies, and yes China has labor protections.</p>

<h4>Musk v OpenAI</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/live/2026/04/30/technology/openai-trial-sam-altman-elon-musk?unlocked_article_code=1.e1A.CCYA.XXncvqeET-UM&amp;smid=nytcore-ios-share">The lawsuit is in its critical phases</a>. <a href="https://trial.mts.now/">Here is a Wiki with statement from the trial</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MikeIsaac/status/2049883094535356454">Rat King has a thread covering Musk’s testimony.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MikeIsaac/status/2049871575772754071">rat king</a>: i am not really sure how often lawyers try to endear themselves to judges but Musk’s lawyer, Steven Molo, does not seem to be trying to do that</p>
<p>right now he’s trying to get “extinction risk” discussion into the court discussion.</p>
<p>“This is a real risk. we all could die.”</p></blockquote>
<p>I mean, he’s not wrong, and I hope Judge Gonzalez is also not wrong here:</p>
<blockquote><p>​<a href="https://x.com/MikeIsaac/status/2049872318613352697">rat king</a>: judge Gonzalez: “I suspect that there are a number of people who do not want to put the future of humanity in Mr. Musk’s hands. But we’re not going to get into that. This is not a trial on the safety risks of artificial intelligence.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Ultimately, yes, we are in the full Don’t Look Up timeline, with lines like this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tbpn/status/2050331770156163218">TBPN</a>: The judge presiding over the OpenAI-Elon trial has prohibited the lawyers from dwelling on doomerism and x-risk.</p>
<p>“She’s like, ‘Look, that’s kind of a sideshow distraction. Extinction of humanity stuff is not the point of this case.'”</p></blockquote>
<p>The judge is technically correct, but yeah, that’s kind of how the world ends, huh?</p>
<p>Here’s a fun fact:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MikeIsaac/status/2049883094535356454">rat king</a>: it is quite significant that Musk admitted on the stand that xAI is distilling OpenAI models to train xAI, and that it is using OpenAI’s technology to build xAI!</p></blockquote>
<p>And another fun (non-AI fact), uh huh, yeah, sure Mr. Musk:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/live/2026/04/30/technology/openai-trial-sam-altman-elon-musk?unlocked_article_code=1.e1A.CCYA.XXncvqeET-UM&amp;smid=nytcore-ios-share">Ryan Mac</a> (NYTimes): Musk said on the stand that he has never directed the algorithm that controls X to promote his own account, but there have been incidences where the company has made changes that favor his account.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MikeIsaac/status/2052053904159084746">Here is another thread, covering Murati’s testimony</a>, which confirms the story that Altman was fired due to concerns about his management of OpenAI, not due to safety concerns.</p>
<p>Here’s another perspective, from former board member Helen Toner.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ZeffMax/status/2052189238444409164">Max Zeff</a>: Helen Toner’s deposition in Musk v Altman includes some striking quotes about Mira Murati’s involvement with Altman’s ouster.</p>
<p>She said Mira was “totally uninterested in telling her team that her conversations with us had been a significant factor” in firing Altman. Also claimed that Mira sat on the fence.</p>
<p>“She [Mira] was waiting to see which way the wind would blow and she didn’t realize that she was the wind.”</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MikeIsaac/status/2052191506543563253">Rat King points out that Satya Nadella seemingly was the only person involved</a> who seems to understand that if you don’t want your conversations read out loud in a court of law, you need to do them in person or on a phone call, not in emails or texts.</p>

<h4></h4>



<h4>Peace In Our Time</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/DKThomp/status/2052066312118047201">Derek Thompson asks the good question</a> of whether this means Elon Musk will stop attacking Anthropic and Dario Amodei. For now, it looks like yes, that Elon Musk decided to take the radical step of actually talking to the Anthropic people and realize that actually they’re not evil after all.</p>
<p>It did seem odd that Elon Musk could keep up this level of animosity to both OpenAI and Anthropic, while those two had such animosity for reach other. That’s not stable.</p>
<p>As always, when someone is fixing a past mistake, you might want to do some amount of ‘hey check out that stupid past mistake you made for dumb reasons’ but mostly you want to say ‘hey congrats and good job on getting it right and changing your mind.’</p>
<p>This is one of those times.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nottombrown/status/2052062566126649448">Tom Brown</a> (Cofounder, Anthropic): In the next few days we’ll be ramping up Claude inference on Colossus.</p>
<p>Grateful to be partnering with SpaceX here. We are going to need to move a lot of atoms in order to keep up with AI demand, and there’s nobody better at quickly moving atoms (on or off planet Earth)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2052069691372478511">Elon Musk</a>: Same here.</p>
<p>By way of background for those who care, I spent a lot of time last week with senior members of the Anthropic team to understand what they do to ensure Claude is good for humanity and was impressed.</p>
<p>Everyone I met was highly competent and cared a great deal about doing the right thing. No one set off my evil detector. So long as they engage in critical self-examination, Claude will probably be good.</p>
<p>After that, I was ok leasing Colossus 1 to Anthropic, as SpaceXAI had already moved training to Colossus 2.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MobofJoggers/status/2052070064946589817">Lincoln</a>: Do you plan on having extra computer to lease out in the future or will SpaceXAI and Tesla be using all of it?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2052076315306864756">Elon Musk</a>: Just as SpaceX launches hundreds of satellites for competitors with fair terms and pricing, we will provide compute to AI companies that are taking the right steps to ensure it is good for humanity.</p>
<p>We reserve the right to reclaim the compute if their AI engages in actions that harm humanity. Doing our best to achieve a great future with amazing abundance for all. We will make mistakes, as to err is human, but always take rapid action to address them.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2052082129048060020">Dean W. Ball</a>: But, but… I thought they were morally depraved purveyors of Woke AI</p>
<p>(Jk; capital in a functioning market will be allocated to its highest and best use, but I do encourage you to remember all the people with supposedly principled opposition to ant who look foolish now)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/S_OhEigeartaigh/status/2052085982749397408">Seán Ó hÉigeartaigh</a>: That’s a principled observation, but if you want America to do well the pragmatist’s answer is to let them all climb down gracefully.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2052086547244036135">Dean W. Ball</a>: Agreed.</p></blockquote>
<p>This move it two things in one. It is Elon Musk hopefully burying a foolish beef and perhaps leading to more cooperation and less fighting, which is good, and reduces race dynamic issues. It is also <a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2052082562408071596">Anthropic getting more compute, which accelerates Anthropic</a> and perhaps means they are teaming up against Altman and OpenAI, and one might reasonably see this as the more important effect and as accelerating race dynamics.</p>


<h4>Quiet Speculations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/natolambert/status/2050610488212627897">There was a lot</a> of this graph going around this week, showing a widening gap between OpenAI and Anthropic in blue, and open Chinese models in red.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2026/05/caisi-evaluation-deepseek-v4-pro">This is from the official CAISI evaluation of DeepSeek v4 Pro</a>, my lord the government’s official Google erasure, it uses many of the usual benchmarks:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/mucw5iza7fluqwjteuvf" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>If you fully believe this graph, v4 just caught up to GPT-5, which puts it 8 months behind with a widening gap. If anything I think this underestimates the gap for the usual reasons.</p>
<p>You could also use other measurements, such as this aggregation of benchmarks from Artificial Analysis. If you look at raw standard benchmarks here you see less of a gap:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/nhy1rzc0lj5yt3gobhry" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2050615021143568555">Dean W. Ball</a>: personally I have found the artificial analysis index to be pretty unrepresentative of what models I enjoy using/benefit from the most.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2050904152511848871">Ethan Mollick</a>: <a href="https://x.com/scaling01/status/2050616057191072161">This is a good explanation</a> of why the gap between open and closed models is larger than it appears in benchmarks. I would add in that current open models are also more fragile than closed: they handle out-of-distribution problems far less well &amp; have lower emergent capabilities.</p></blockquote>
<p>Dean is giving us the nice version. The not as nice version is that the AA-style benchmarks are being gamed, check the particular areas of focus of open models, are disproportionately impacted by distillation strategies, and are only meaningful as part of setting a gestalt and overall context.</p>
<p>As Lisan points out, there’s also the additional delay that closed model companies face when they do safety testing and other prep work prior to a release, whereas the open model companies, despite not being able to undo a release, mostly just yolo.</p>


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>


<p>The reaction to this finding that we are likely a few years away from probably all dying does not seem to be ‘oh looks like we are all only a few years away from probably dying and we should do something about that.’</p>
<p>The ‘why this matters’ section of his post does not even seem to raise this implication and danger. A hell of a missing mood.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jackclarkSF/status/2051312759594471886">Jack Clark</a> (Anthropic): I’ve spent the past few weeks reading 100s of public data sources about AI development.<a href="https://importai.substack.com/p/import-ai-455-automating-ai-research?r=1ds20&amp;utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;triedRedirect=true"> I now believe that recursive self-improvement has a 60% chance of happening by the end of 2028.</a> In other words, AI systems might soon be capable of building themselves.</p>
<p>… A lot of the conclusion comes from assembling a mosaic out of many distinct data sources. Some examples – progress on CORE-Bench, where the task is implementing other research papers (huge amounts of AI research comes from interpreting and replicating results)</p>
<p>My whole experience doing this project was finding endless “up and to the right” graphs at all resolutions of AI R&amp;D, from the well known (e.g., SWE-Bench) to more niche (like those above). It’s a fractal, but at all the resolutions you see the same trend of meaningful progress.</p></blockquote>
<p>Jack basically says that <a href="https://x.com/alexolegimas/status/2051298253757460783">even with only unglamorous ‘meat and potatoes</a>’ innovations you can get to critical mass for such advances. I think that is correct. The people saying ‘AI will never have a new idea’ are being silly, but the disagreement is not even load bearing here.</p>
<p>Some people are <a href="https://x.com/binarybits/status/2051326666266362060">remarkably dense about what this means</a> in another sense, as if the computer not doing the physical construction would matter in this scenario. It wouldn’t.</p>
<p>Here’s another opinion, which still boils down to ‘that’s stupidly soon, yikes’:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RyanPGreenblatt/status/2051373130804011512">Ryan Greenblatt</a>: I think the chance of AIs capable of fully automating AI R&amp;D by the end of 2028 is around 30%. So I expect things to take a bit longer than Jack does, but not by that much and timelines as fast as Jack is imagining seem totally plausible to me.</p></blockquote>


<h4></h4>



<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/ai-companies-can-t-regulate-themselves-they-should-regulate-each-other">Could an AI SRO (self-regulatory organization) allow the labs to regulate each other</a>? Mark Thomas finds it promising. I am skeptical, but I am certainly in favor of the law allowing the labs to try and removing any fears of antitrust issues, as this does not rule out other actions.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2051357900472275017">What did this new Maryland law (HB 895) do</a>? Did it ban a broad range of ‘dynamic pricing’ strategies in harmful ways?</p>
<ol>
<li>For large grocery retailers and third-party food delivery providers, minimum size 15k sq ft, it bans using personalized data to set prices.
<ol>
<li>I think this is good. Personalized price changes force you to be in a state of constant adversarial information war and paranoia, and end up wasting everyone’s time.</li>
<li>There is a lot of value in being able to simply be a price taker.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>This carves out a wide variety of established methods of offering dynamic prices.
<ol>
<li>If anything I think the carve-out is too broad from a full welfare perspective, but on libertarian grounds I’m fine with it.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>If you set a price using personal data beyond standard carve outs like employee discounts, you have to tell the customer.
<ol>
<li>Again, that seems actively good, because it allows consumers to relax about personal data and trust that they are price takers.</li>
<li>In a sense, this imposes the cost of dynamic pricing on the dynamic pricer, since it means I notice and can respond accordingly.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p>I do think a lot of laws that look similar will end up being too restrictive, and I’m not sure where the line is <a href="https://truthonthemarket.com/2025/12/10/instacart-didnt-read-your-mind-it-ran-an-a-b-test/">(more discussion here)</a>, but these rules in particular seem fine.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_271828/status/2052042382666023059">Alex Bores now in a dead heat for NY-12</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2052228328065581107">Congressman Greg Casar agrees with Bernie Sanders</a> that if there’s a 10% chance humanity could be destroyed by uncontrolled AI, we should do everything possible to prevent it. That’s a more extreme position than I have, as I think we should do many things but not ‘everything possible.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2052115293183398321">Connecticut introduces a new AI bill with some new provisions</a>, that <a href="https://ctnewsjunkie.com/2026/05/02/bill-regulating-ai-heads-to-lamonts-desk-after-bipartisan-house-passage/?utm_source=CTNewsJunkie+Newsletter+Group&amp;utm_campaign=6af9cd4b05-ON_THE_HOUR&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_term=0_a493d2308d-6af9cd4b05-95967709">looks like it is through to the governor</a>. As per Peter Wildeford’s notes:</p>
<ol>
<li>A voluntary auditing program for catastrophic risk</li>
<li>Whistleblower protections.</li>
<li>Child safety screentime protections including ‘75% of screen, 30 seconds, undismissable on first daily access’ with later follow-ups. That’s pretty obnoxious, and I don’t see how it helps other than by being obnoxious. Risk of backfire if it means you wake up and load the AI program so you can get through the warning.</li>
<li>Bans various behaviors in context of a child user.</li>
<li>Employers must provide notice when using AI in hiring, including listing ‘tool name, purpose, data categories, sources, contact info.’</li>
<li>If layoffs are AI-related you have to tell CT Labor Department.</li>
<li>Mandatory watermarking for major platforms with carve-outs.</li>
<li>A model regulation working group.</li>
</ol>
<p>The rule about AI use in hiring decisions seems like the kind of thing where you first say ‘AI, write me the disclosure notice’ but also this idea of ‘data categories’ illustrates how much they don’t get what is happening here. Presumably having to disclose the tool will push corporations to use standard tools to avoid questions.</p>


<h4>People Really Hate AI</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AlexSJacquez/status/2050931003846234379">Alex Jacquez</a>: That AI number is a big fat jump ball for Dems to seize</p>
<p>Senator <a href="https://x.com/ChrisMurphyCT/status/2051278816408981619">Chris Murphy</a> (D-Connecticut): Being the party that will protect people from the worst of AI is the right thing to do and has the side benefit of being very politically advantageous.</p></blockquote>
<p>When asked, most people don’t trust either party on AI, and Democrats despite their populist objections and generally more anti-AI stance haven’t won any trust. There’s still a big opportunity here. Keeping the issue nonpartisan to the extent possible would be first best, but was always unlikely in the long term, so while things are staying less partisan than I feared for longer than I hoped, it likely won’t last forever.</p>


<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2049924785153638761">Epoch estimates 20%-60% of China’s total compute is from illegal smuggled chips</a>, which is ~<a href="https://epoch.ai/blog/chip-smuggling">3% of all global compute</a>.</p>

<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7wy3xyoXYt8&amp;t=1s">Rational animations offers a basic primer on existential risk</a>, Yudkowsky style. Yudkowsky thinks they did a good job here.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Hx5dIJ3H8p4">Odd Lots on METR and their famous graph</a>, and <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lxmt7rz4DLQ">on the Taiwan situation</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=A2x639ist6s">Peter Wildeford on FLI’s podcast.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://www.npr.org/2026/04/21/g-s1-118208/a-new-npr-network-podcast-asks-the-big-question-are-we-doomed">NPR asks, are we doomed</a>? <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ap5qlqhROWg&amp;t=1s">In particular, from AI</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/liron/status/2051831569770336551">If you pay $10,000</a> y<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FIg4zQKBpAs">ou too can debate</a> Eliezer Yudkowsky and yell at him to shut up. Getting him to take you seriously will cost extra. The googles? Priceless.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/xlr8harder/status/2051912812566360330">xlr8harder</a>: I’m not going to watch any debate but I hope one outcome from this is that we collectively begin exploring the boundaries of what @alltheyud will do for $10,000.</p>
<p>This is a scenario in which everyone wins.</p></blockquote>
