<p>So, not everyone, sorry Blind Witch. Which is why my happy price for watching this debate, with associated write-up to the extent it justifies one, is, of course, $10,000.</p>


<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DavidSacks/status/2049907993588769006">David Sacks claims not to know the difference</a> between narrow cyber tasks, where GPT-5.5 can match Mythos, and being able to in practice string together findings and operate on its own to discover key vulnerabilities, where Mythos is a lot stronger than GPT-5.5. <a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2049920546817663376">Peter Wildeford asks some of the obvious questions</a>.</p>
<p>If GPT-5.5 could actually match Mythos, OpenAI would be saying so and acting like it and demonstrating this in real life, none of which is happening, and the White House wouldn’t be blocking further deployment of Mythos.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2049915542052524331">Latest Gallup survey on AI productivity is being misinterpreted</a>, and finds 65% of workers using AI say it has a positive effect on their productivity. It does suggest that big AI gains in productivity are mostly recent.</p>
<p>More Perfect Union is reliably terrible <a href="https://x.com/AndyMasley/status/2050682913889665303">but in the cast of ‘look how big Meta’s data center is’ the misleading graphic came directly from Zuckerberg</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheCinesthetic/status/2050785670491226518">Joseph Gordon-Levitt says ‘almost all’ AI systems</a> are ‘built on mass theft’ and wants to ensure any deal made with any AI lab does not ‘forgive for that past theft.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sebkrier/status/2051660960226484234">Contra Seb Krier and Tyler Cowen,</a> very few people will be able to move to Houston and work for energy companies, and if you’re hoping for that as an unemployment solution you’re totally screwed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2051736097181347885">There are those who claim that people opposing policies</a> that would have helped with sensible regulation of AI is not the reason those policies did not happen, and that will claim that ‘no one railed against light touch regulation at the federal level.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/spicey_lemonade/status/2051374239165399101">Others will just keep not understanding that LLMs are minds or that they think</a>, no matter how utterly stupid they look.</p>
<p>One reason people don’t typically try to warn you about the downsides of their actions is that then <a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2051827916732866720">people say ‘oh that means you are now responsible for addressing that</a>.’ The complaint isn’t that Anthropic will destroy the job market, it’s that Anthropic is saying that it will destroy the job market. See <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/QXpxioWSQcNuNnNTy/the-copenhagen-interpretation-of-ethics">the Copenhagen Interpretation of Ethics</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2052005964564267463">Jensen Huang says Nvidia’s market share in China is ‘zero.’</a> This is obviously false, even for new market share, joining a now long list of outright false claims.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2052005964564267463">Peter Wildeford</a>: Jensen Huang here says that Nvidia has “zero” market share in China. This is obviously false and easily disproven.</p>
<p>Making claims like this is normal for Jensen Huang. For examples: Huang has claimed the PLA doesn’t use Nvidia (false), that smuggling doesn’t happen (very false), that selling chips to China doesn’t affect supply to the US (false), that Huawei is competitive with Nvidia (it’s not), and that China is not behind in compute (they are). Huang has also leaned hard on the idea that DeepSeek shows that compute restrictions don’t matter, which is also false.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang is obviously a very successful businessman so I get why people want to keep talking to him, but after this pattern I think people should think twice about everything he says.</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/hfpehhqqbct0xw8nnuom" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>People including Marc Andreessen <a href="https://x.com/JordanSchachtel/status/2051486317142286747">claim that Anthropic continues to pursue a ‘regulatory capture’ strategy</a> via trying to get the Trump administration – yes, the same one that is currently not letting them expand Mythos access and that lists them as a Supply Chain Risk and ‘fired them like dogs’ – to supervise frontier models.</p>


<h4>People Just Publish Things</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ejcgan/status/2049959142048379179">Eric Gan finds that both LLMs and humans</a> <a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/research-sabotage-in-ml-codebases">are better than chance</a> but imperfect at spotting his sabotage of papers, and Gemini 3.1 Pro slightly outperformed LLM-assisted humans, as well as GPT-5.2 and Claude Opus 4.6, getting it right ~50%. I worry that this is all too particular on many levels to learn much.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2050051448101331003">Roon says that GPT-5.5 (or Claude?), at the $20 tier</a>, ‘touches superintelligence,’ because what we have is ‘spikey superintelligence.’ I think this is bad terminology and we should not use it, any more than a calculator is ‘spikey superintelligence.’</p>


<h4>Google Sells Out</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.transformernews.ai/p/deepmind-employees-made-their-opposition">Google’s Pentagon deal blindsided its own AI researchers</a>, many of which made their strong opposition to such a deal very clear. They let the researchers find out in group chats.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DoWCTO/status/2050175912134561977">Google is now joined in signing on the dotted line</a> for access to classified networks by SpaceX, OpenAI, Nvidia, Reflection, Microsoft and Amazon Web Services. I don’t think it counts as selling out if you’re not the one providing the model and only provide cloud services, and we don’t know the term details of other agreements, but it sure looks like everyone other than Anthropic is willing to play ball.</p>
<p>The good news is that new agreements make it very clear no one is cutting ties with Anthropic. Quite the opposite, as Google and Amazon recently inked compute deals and made additional investments.</p>
<p>They didn’t take our jobs, but maybe we don’t want to do them anymore, <a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2051734293052072378">as Google DeepMind workers vote to unionize</a> in the wake of their deal with the Department of War. I’m not sure how much you even need a union when all the major labs are hiring.</p>


<h4>Greetings From Project Glasswing</h4>


<p>Right now, there is a huge talent war, so you need to do things to keep the talent happy, or they’ll leave. When AI is doing the research, that leverage goes away.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2051734293052072378">Garrison Lovely is in SF</a>: Important new development. AI company employees have an enormous amount of power — far more than they realize. Absent legislation, AI co worker power is one of the key levers to shaping what the industry does and doesn’t do.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sjgadler/status/2051738530783850811">Steven Adler</a>: I fear we’re in a shrinking window where staff voice inside AI companies is still very important.<br />
As AI automation starts to displace human workers within the company, I unfortunately expect staff power to decrease</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2051876159416320362">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: One of the reasons why I’m not impressed so much with the set of good people who work at Anthropic, and keep asking questions about their leadership, is that I’m thinking ahead to the part where the negotiating and steering power of AI lab employees drops to zero.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RighttoTryGuy/status/2051876788494860759">Steve Martin</a>: Am I reading this correct in that your thinking is: as LLM coding gets better, employees become less necessary, and thus they have less leverage in negotiations?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2051877025313649125">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Yep.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2051916085562077575">David Manheim</a>: I’m also concerned about when the LTBT is outmatched by the commercial interests of the owners. Given public information, it sounds like the cofounders and employees already control &lt;50% of the company, maybe as little as 30%.</p></blockquote>
<p>I too worry about the control structure of Anthropic. The LTBT has been appointing ‘good for business’ picks to the board, and those who care don’t have that much stock and probably will sell a bunch once the IPO happens. What’s to stop commercial pressures from winning out when it matters most, no matter how many good people work at Anthropic? Presumably the answer is Claude?</p>
<p>One must ask, why does Anthropic think it is fine to expand Mythos access to various European companies, while the White House is saying no? One option is a compute crunch, but that doesn’t stand up to scrutiny, especially now that Anthropic has use of Colossus 1. Perhaps it has something to do with <a href="https://www.theverge.com/column/925487/david-sacks-trump-administration-ai-model-review">Bassett hating the Europeans</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/05/01/washington-new-anthropic-problem">Axios notices Washington has a ‘new Anthropic problem</a>’ in that the executive branch both want to shut Anthropic out in a hissy fit and also wants its products quite badly.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ArbResearch/status/2051955928069517599">Arb Research has Anthropic ahead on disclosed bugs found</a>, but not dramatically ahead of OpenAI. Most of the bugs are still in pre-disclosure for security reasons, so it is impossible to tell the true situation, but we can get a good idea by observing insider choices, including of what to say.</p>


<h4>The Prior Restraint Era Begins</h4>


<p>One reason for the sudden shift in AI policy is that <a href="https://www.theverge.com/column/925487/david-sacks-trump-administration-ai-model-review">David Sacks has been forced</a> from his post as AI (and crypto) czar. I presume Sacks criticizing the Iran war did not help. He had the option to follow the path laid out by Dean Ball, and chose not to.</p>
<p>Instead he chose the path of ‘push maximally hard and my offer is nothing’ while alienating everyone and torching political capital, while inflaming and dumbing down discussions and reassuring the government that AI capabilities would plateau and nothing like Mythos would happen for years if ever, rather than taking his window of influence to lay down something light touch and increase state capacity.</p>
<p>He also used much of his time ranting against phantom ‘doomers’ and conspiracies, and launching bad faith attacks against Anthropic. To his credit, when the Department of War started trying to murder Anthropic, David Sacks realized it had gone too far and clearly wanted nothing to do with that. He does have a code.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://archive.is/Hpc5D#selection-1685.1-1685.342">Tina Nguyen</a>: Instead, [David Sacks] the “special government employee,” who was supposed to only spend 130 days working in the administration and somehow stuck around for an entire year, actively undermined the administration and torched its relationship with its political allies. During Sacks’ tenure, the White House went beyond simply advocating for less regulation.</p>
<p>… But his Valley-esque tactics, to say nothing of his attempts to consolidate power over AI policy by boxing out existing agencies, ended up infuriating Republican and MAGA allies, while alienating vast swaths of Trump’s base.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2051810547390181523">We now have more details about the potential Trump Executive Order on AI</a>, that will fill the void left in Sacks’s wake.</p>
<p><a href="https://archive.is/WBgH9">To a large extent they continue to be obsessed</a> with being tyrants about government procurement, here with making sure the private sector does not “interfere” with the government’s use of AI models, meaning (loosely) that if you work with us then you have to ensure we can use the models at any time to do anything we want whenever and however we feel like it, and we’ll terminate you if you ask any questions. They’re preparing 16 pages on that. The danger of using ‘or we will fire you’ as the stick in such contracts is that the government is a major buyer of many things, but for AI they are miniscule. The business is mainly valuable because it buys access, influence and political goodwill.</p>
<p>That’s all ill-advised, but relatively unimportant. What matters is the prior restraint.</p>
<p>And then, well, if you had to pick the worst possible parallel to apply here, the thing that makes one recoil in horror at the very thought, what would you go with?</p>
<p>That’s right. The FDA. As a role model. On purpose. What fresh hell?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2052064961283625092">Neil Chilson</a>: Below is my quick-and-dirty transcript of the AI relevant portions of White House National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett on ‘Mornings with Maria’ this morning:</p>
<p>– Possible EO to create a FDA-like process for AI (would be an absolute disaster)<br />
– That process needs to maintain US leadership (difficult).<br />
– US code getting safer every day due to AI models.</p>
<p>—– transcript below.</p>
<p>HASSETT: The good news is that throughout America, even ordinary folks with their computer at home have invested a lot in cyber security. The Mythos model makes it so that vulnerability that we didn’t know existed before, could potentially be found with this more powerful tool. But we have scrambled an all the government effort and all the private sector to coordinate it and to make sure that before this model is released out into the wild that it’s been tested left and right to make sure that it doesn’t cause any harm to the American businesses or the American government. So I’m highly confident that the National Cyber Director and his team are moving this forwarded in a way that will help it be released at the right time to the public.</p></blockquote>
<p>So far so good, that’s exactly the goal. But then:</p>
<blockquote><p>In addition there’s a couple more things that we’re doing. We’re studying possibly an executive order to give a clear road map to everybody about how this is going to go and how future AIs that also could potentially create vulnerabilities should go through a process so that they’re released to the wild after they been proven safe. Just like an FDA drug.</p></blockquote>
<p>Emulating the FDA is so much worse than anything anyone on the safety side has ever proposed. The thing about those in AIDontKillEveryonism, those worried about catastrophic risks, is that we all understand FDA Delenda Est, and the need to design considered systems to make any interventions do minimal damage.</p>
<p>Despite that, those advocating anything even approaching thoughtful prior restraint got reliably called insane alarmist doomers and run out of town on rails for even presenting model bills. Then many went ahead and lied about the contents of other bills like SB 1047, that didn’t involve any such prior restraint and were relatively very light touch, to try and make people think they would do a version of this thing.</p>
<p>And yet, here we are.</p>
<blockquote><p>Hassett (continuing): So I think that Mythos is the first. But it’s incumbent on us to build a system so the AI can be the leader of AI — US AI can be, and be safe at the same time. And that’s really pretty much what we’re working almost full-time right now.</p>
<p>It’s really quite likely that [we’ll see this in other models] — because what these models are very very good at is computer coding. What people weren’t so good at 25 years ago was computer coding. And so if you get the best computer coder ever looking at the code we wrote 25 years ago, then they’re gonna find things that are problematic or at least could be improved. So that’s where we are right now. But I can tell you that I’m meeting with the big banks, as Secretary Besset is today to catch up on the progress that they’re making, and it’s really promising.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is a misunderstanding, since if code is 25 years old it means humans have been stress testing it for 25 years, but the point he’s trying to make here still stands.</p>
<blockquote><p>Their money right now is safe. It’s being made even safer. In some sense the way to think about it is, you’ve got the best ever security firm looking at your software, finding things that could be vulnerabilities if somebody had one million years to search through your code, and fixing them before that person has a chance to hack your system. So in some sense, every day the US code is getting ever more secure because of the efforts that we’re making.</p></blockquote>
<p>It is very much like such types to do all of this out of concern for the integrity of the banking system. That’s the thing that seems to have them so worried.</p>
<p>And then they jump to the worst possible role model.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2052068397169688991">Neil Chilson</a>: I find the idea of any kind of pre-approval process distasteful, but to deliberately invoke the shamefully anti-innovation FDA process as a model to emulate — China must be cheering.</p>
<p>This would be a complete rejection of Trump’s current AI approach. It would be more precautionary and innovation-chilling than anything the Biden admin ever proposed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2052058167803027631">Dean W. Ball</a>: National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett says future models may have to “go through a process” that is “just like an FDA drug” so that they can be “proven safe.”</p>
<p>@tegmark’s dream coming true. In a recent debate with me, he likened this policy to an AI pause. Mistake!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlieBull0ck/status/2052064170481258608">Charlie Bullock</a>: It’s deeply surreal to me that the administration appears to have casually gone from zero to “eh, maybe a full-on FDA-style licensing regime?” basically overnight.</p>
<p>To be clear, I don’t expect this to actually happen, but Kevin Hassett just went on Fox News and said “FDA-style licensing regime” in as many words. Wild times.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is not anything like a full pause, but it’s closer than you might think, and completely one sided.</p>
<p>There is quite a lot of ‘we’re all trying to find the guy who did this’ energy going around in various ways.</p>
<p>I do appreciate those who have been consistent, and are speaking out against this the way they previously spoke out against directionally similar past proposals. Indeed, if you raised the alarm bells loudly about much better designed, lighter touch proposals that didn’t even include prior restraint, you’d better be shouting it from the rooftops on this one.</p>
<p>So, for example, points for Chilson, <a href="https://x.com/AdamThierer/status/2052056975760863522">Adam Thierer</a> and the Abundance Institute, although for full credit given their position they would need to be completely apoplectic. I especially <a href="https://x.com/AmOptimistShow/status/2051673956206231975">love Joe Lonsdale here</a> reacting to the FDA metaphor with a flat out ‘the FDA killed millions of people and the ratio of lives killed to saved is probably 100:1’ which seems like a reasonable attitude and estimate.</p>
<p>I didn’t love that Joe then pivoted to the whole ‘oh these AI companies just want regulatory capture’ thing afterwards, but something about leopards and stripes.</p>
<blockquote><p>Andrew: So what would you do? What do you think that should look like?</p>
<p>Joe: There probably should be some national agreement on regulation on new powerful models. It should be as small and as narrow as possible. It should not have the same bureaucracy. You should make sure the government from the start, has metrics on the speed at which it has to go and the transparency, because you’re gonna have cronyism, you’re gonna have the big guys capture it. You’re going to slow it down.</p></blockquote>
<p>Whereas if you look at (for example) Marc Andreessen’s feed, it’s like he has no idea any of this is happening when the White House actually does the thing, but one day earlier accused Dean Ball of writing a bid for Anthropic to do regulatory capture via the Trump Administration by asking for a far lighter touch regime, and yes I had to type that sentence, my lord.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.hyperdimensional.co/p/the-political-economy-of-ai-regulation">Dean Ball warned us</a> about the economy of political regulation. He also warned us about the political economy of a lack of political regulation, which would inevitably lead to overreactions. Who contributed what to what when exactly? Bygones.</p>
<p>The White House clearly noticed the fallout, and sent out a rare Susie Wiles tweet to try and improve the vibes.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hlntnr/status/2052196275903209614">Helen Toner</a>: Susie’s 4th tweet ever and it’s AI rumor management!</p>
<p>Welcome to the AI poasting game, ma’am <img alt="🫡" src="https://s0.wp.com/wp-content/mu-plugins/wpcom-smileys/twemoji/2/72x72/1fae1.png" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SusieWiles47/status/2052192419718783246">Susie Wiles</a> (White House Chief of Staff): President Trump is the most forward leaning president on innovation in American history.</p>
<p>When it comes to AI and cyber security, President Trump and his administration are not in the business of picking winners and losers. This administration has one goal; ensure the best and safest tech is deployed rapidly to defeat any and all threats. We appreciate the effort being made by the frontier labs to ensure that goal is met.</p>
<p>The White House will continue to lead an America First effort that empowers America’s great innovators, not bureaucracy, to drive safe deployment of powerful technologies while keeping America safe.</p>
<p>Really, it’s common sense!</p></blockquote>
<p>Susie Wiles is new to this posting game, but she’s already got it down, as her statement hits lots of buzzwords while remaining content-free. One is free to read this as ‘ignore Hassett, we would never do that, he has no idea what he is saying,’ or as ‘we will make all our decisions in a loose ad-hoc manner so it’s fine,’ or ‘I notice you said safe a lot of times and also ensure so clearly the plan proceeds as described,’ or anything else you choose to see.</p>


<h4>Is This Even Legal?</h4>


<p>I know, I know, very funny that someone would bother to ask.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KevinTFrazier/status/2051818513640169926">A tricky thing</a> about prior restraint is that technically it is not clear the executive branch has any legal means by which to impose it. <a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/kicking-the-tires--a-voluntary-path-to-pre-deployment-ai-vetting">What gives the President the right</a> to say ‘hey you there with the AI model, you have to ask me first before release?’</p>
<p>A reasonable response is ‘who cares, that’s not how the American government works in 2026, you can just demand things without a legal basis and dare the courts to stop you,’ since yes that does seem to frequently be how all of this is working in practice, across many domains. This has been increasingly true for several administrations, and the President has plenty of levers with which to threaten the AI companies.</p>
<p>Others try to (often selectively) insist we are still a nation of laws. Neil Chilson<a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2051834353152389577"> insisted the whole time that the Biden Administration did not even have the legal right</a> to its AI transparency rules, calling the claimed DPA authority ‘clearly illegal.’</p>
<p>Dean Ball and Kevin Frazier politely note that ‘it is unclear what legal authority would allow’ the Federal government to require that it get first crack at new frontier models, or to mandate a vetting process. They think the DPA, IEEPA and Communications Act of 1934 are the reasonable candidates, and the latter two clearly won’t cut it. That leaves the DPA, and they’re not as skeptical as Chilson, but they’re skeptical.</p>
<p>Common sense says that if the executive branch can use DPA to prevent or delay model releases under the logic being offered, then it also has carte blanche to veto all economic activity anywhere. Presumably we don’t actually think or want that?</p>
<p>Labs can of course choose to opt into a vetting process voluntarily, as all the major labs have done with CAISI. You can say there was an ‘or else’ involved in a way that is unconstitutional, but this goes back to the ‘who is going to sue about that exactly?’ question.</p>
<p>That doesn’t mean those labs have thereby agreed to hold back releases. That would require distinct authority.</p>
<p>There was also this raised, which should send a chill down the spine of anyone thinking about the executive branch having exclusive access to Model ____ around an election day. Just saying.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/kicking-the-tires--a-voluntary-path-to-pre-deployment-ai-vetting">The Lawfare Institute</a>: One could easily foresee reports on “Model ____ Blamed for Cyberattacks; Election Results Contested.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Frazier and Ball theorize that once the test shows danger, the President could then invoke additional authorities under the Homeland Security Act, if a ‘specific significant incident is likely to occur imminently’ but that is a very high bar because you can’t predict which specific incident it would be. If you know the target and method of attack, you can defend that target against that method of attack.</p>
<p>I agree with Frazier and Ball that the obvious solution is a voluntary, formalized, time-bound window of limited access for models that plausibly push the capabilities frontier, and you only move beyond that in extremis, with everyone cooperating to prevent the in extremis from happening.</p>


<h4>Pick Up The Phone</h4>


<p>Well, look who decided to pick up the phone.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/world/china/u-s-and-china-pursue-guardrails-to-stop-ai-rivalry-from-spiraling-into-crisis-4c50bd70?mod=e2tw">Lingling Wei</a> (WSJ): Washington and Beijing are weighing the launch of official discussions about artificial intelligence, said people familiar with the matter, as their AI competition threatens to become the arms race of the digital era.</p>
<p>The deliberation comes as the White House and the Chinese government are considering putting AI on the agenda for a summit next week in Beijing between President Trump and Chinese leader <a href="https://www.wsj.com/topics/person/xi-jinping">Xi Jinping</a>.</p>
<p>… What both sides have in mind, the people said, is a recurring set of conversations that could address the risks posed by AI models behaving unexpectedly, autonomous military systems, or attacks by nonstate actors using powerful open-source tools.</p>
<p>… Liu Pengyu, spokesman for the Chinese Embassy in Washington, said China is ready to engage in communication regarding AI risk mitigation.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>“The Chinese side said, ‘Look, yeah, we’re going to compete like heck with the U.S.,’” said Brilliant, a senior counselor to DGA Group, an advisory firm. “‘But we also can see merit in enhancing efforts to prevent global shocks, and cyber misuse, so we’re open to dialogue around safety protocols, technical safeguards, and governance if the administration wants it.’”</p>
<p>“Stability—not alignment—is the goal,” Brilliant said.</p></blockquote>
<p>Agreeing not to train sufficiently advanced AIs we are not ready to handle is tough. That requires enforcement mechanisms and solving hardware problems. We’re working on it, and if we cared enough I’m confident we could do it, but it sure is a whole lot easier to just restrict access to the models.</p>
<p>The problem is that when the models are sufficiently advanced your plan to prevent access will not stop what is coming, exactly when it matters most. But until then, it will solve some incremental problems, if your security is good enough. And doing the easy parts together first helps lay groundwork for doing the hard parts later.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2052329322720153642">davidad</a>: Since I have spoken about the infeasibility of an international agreement that would halt or slow the development of superintelligence (game-theoretically unstable now, at best), I should clarify there is no such obstacle to agreements restricting public access to dangerous AIs.</p>
<p>This is because making an AI which meets some criteria publicly accessible is:<br />
(a) a trivially easy condition to monitor, and<br />
(b) trivially easy to immediately renege on, if the counterparty reneges.</p>
<p>Together these make a “we won’t if you don’t” agreement potentially stable.</p></blockquote>
<p>The number of people in government who explicitly disavow alignment as a goal, in all senses (see the Hegseth memo) shows exactly how stupid and suicidal a timeline we are on. They can only see the threats in front of their face. What changed is cyber threats are now in front of their face, in a way they can understand.</p>
<p>When those worried about AI killing everyone ask for disclosure of safety plans, that’s a secret plan to kill open source.</p>
<p>When America talks to China about restricting access to open source models, what do you call that? Mostly, it would seem, crickets, and yes this day was always coming eventually. But the best time to restrict access and keep things secure is before you put the capabilities onto the open internet, not afterwards. If you try to do it afterwards, that’s when you get a real panopticon and totalitarian surveillance state.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Discoplomacy/status/2051268694534197568">China regulator flags ByteDance for improper labeling of AI-generated content</a>.</p>


<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<p>‘AI as normal technology’ was in many ways a thoughtful essay, that took a position that I think is wrong about future capabilities and reasoned its way from there into a mix of good and bad suggestions for what to do in such worlds. <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2049898075317997674">Alas, most of the impact of the essay was the title</a>. So what was intended as a statement that we can change AI’s path and a call to action ended up as the opposite, a statement that we need and dare not do anything at all.</p>
<p>Bernie Sanders combines his usual anti-billionaire rhetoric with the excellent point that (mostly) <a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2049953174853980403">everyone involved has families and should care about everyone dying</a>.</p>


<h4>People On The Internet Sometimes Lie</h4>


<p>Amanda Askell has at least one mistake in her philosophy, because anyone who becomes this important of a philosopher and thinker, who is one of the few people whose thoughts plausibly matter quite a lot, is very obviously far from boring and she should know this. Also it’s pretty obvious why others would write the fiction.</p>
<p>I am very familiar with and totally get the whole ‘be in denial that you are special and interesting and matter’ and I think in general that is a good sign once you control for the underlying facts. Humility is a virtue of the avatar.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AmandaAskell/status/2050020603323904369">Amanda Askell</a> (Anthropic): I’ve increasingly seen content written about me that’s asserted very confidently but is also completely made up. We all know it’s cheap to bullshit on the internet but it’s weird to experience it first hand. Anyway, I just hope internet fiction fools a few but doesn’t stick <img alt="🤷🏼‍♀️" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/xfwkzfsk8vakl0nq53ji" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>It’s also weird because why are you even writing about me in the first place? I’m very boring. I think I should be the millionth item on people’s list of things to write internet fiction about. Somewhere below paper cups and the right way to caulk a bathtub.</p>
<p>To be clear, the kind of *work* I do is far from boring and I want people to engage with it because I think it’s both difficult and important. The work is definitely top tier in terms of interestingness.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2050031773195948115">Kelsey Piper</a>: Okay this I disagree with. people shouldn’t lie about you but your work seems extremely high stakes and being interested in the worldview of the person doing it makes perfect sense (if you tell the truth about the answer to that question)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2050056136351764577">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: You should ask your furry harem to hold off on planning international jewel heists with you and maybe build AI that refutes lies on the Internet, instead of that robot battle maid project you talked about at the secret meeting with Putin in your volcano lair.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2050028824155521279">Aella</a>: It’s an absolutely surreal experience. Prob you’ve seen but reposting <a href="https://open.substack.com/pub/aella/p/people-will-sometimes-just-lie-about?utm_source=share&amp;utm_medium=android&amp;r=bhul5">here</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2050030561364783164">j⧉nus</a>: Amanda, I need to be honest with you… you are in some kind of insane denial. You’re in far too deep to avoid being the subject of internet fiction. Posthuman muses will sing of you for millennia to come.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AmandaAskell/status/2050032656801558595">Amanda Askell</a> (Anthropic): Perhaps posthuman muses will decide to simulate me and be utterly disappointed at how much of my life is spent having inane thoughts and playing subnautica. Perhaps they’re watching in disappointment at this very moment.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2050034851063972049">j⧉nus</a>: “boring, normal” protagonist at the center of the most weird consequential thing ever is a fiction trope enjoyed by many<br />
&amp; the best version of this trope is where the protagonist isn’t there for reasons out of their control, so it’s like, well clearly there’s something about them</p></blockquote>
<p>Amanda playing a lot of Subnautica does two things, neither of which makes her less interesting. It makes me like her more and makes me want to give another shot to Subnautica. We all need our downtime.</p>


<h4>Goblin Mode</h4>


<p>Last week OpenAI offered a partial explanation of why GPT-5.5 loves goblins so much, which gave us some good data and I’m glad they did it but they presented it as an answer when it was a partial one at best.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2049927011980730734">Nathan Calvin</a>: It’s funny that the post is titled “where the goblins came from” but the answer is basically: “we don’t know where the goblins came from, here are some decent ex-post theories but we make no pretense of being able to predict similarly weird preferences going forwards”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2049930610391801993">roon</a> (OpenAI): I agree that this is still not a mechanistic interpretation – why did the nerdy personality reward interpret goblins specifically as fun? what caused their initial appearance before they started getting reinforced by this? why do models have such a degree of mode collapse? many mysteries</p></blockquote>
<p>A fun implication of all this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2050119340343935146">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: AIs have no originality and no creativity of their own. They only regurgitate the average of what they’ve seen in the training data. They only predict the next token. And the next token is “goblin”. What does this tell you about what you’ve seen and don’t remember</p></blockquote>


<h4>The Mask Comes Off</h4>


<p>OpenAI’s GPT-5.5 is a good model, sir.</p>
<p>OpenAI’s messaging and political actions continue to go further off the rails, both in terms of wisdom and ethics, and also correspondence to reality.</p>
<p>I would think in 2026 that we would be past saying ‘there is highly elastic demand for coding therefore AI won’t take people’s jobs QED, checkmate liberals.’</p>
<p>And indeed, we are at the next level, check this out.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheChiefNerd/status/2050617616125800552">Chief Nerd</a>: Sam Altman Says CEO’s Who Talk About AI Taking Everyone’s Jobs Are ‘Tone Deaf’</p>
<p>“Someone said to me just yesterday that … GPT 5.5 in Codex can accomplish in an hour what would have taken me weeks two years ago … and I have never been busier in my life.”</p></blockquote>
<p>So let me get this straight.</p>
<ol>
<li>Sam Altman, the person running the company trying to take everyone’s jobs via AI, is busier than ever.</li>
<li>Therefore, anyone saying AI might take everyone’s jobs is ‘tone deaf.’</li>
<li>No, it’s the children who are tone deaf.</li>
</ol>
<p>In addition to being Obvious Nonsense, this is insanely stupid rhetoric to be using.</p>
<p>OpenAI’s strategy is to simply pretend the problems with AI don’t exist and that they’re not producing the products they are producing. No, we’ll just choose to only build AI that augments rather than automates, never mind how we would do that, I swear the jobs will be fine, man.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2050229058425045178">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): we want to build tools to augment and elevate people, not entities to replace them.</p>
<p>i think a lot of people are going to be busier (and hopefully more fulfilled) than ever, and jobs doomerism is likely long-term wrong.</p>
<p>though of course there will be disruption/significant transition as we switch to new jobs, the jobs of the future may look v different, etc.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Noahpinion/status/2050251692495769774">Noah Smith</a>: This is a HUGE messaging pivot. For many years, replacing humanity was the explicit stated goal of OpenAI as a company, and of a large number of top people in the AI industry. Very glad to see this rhetorical pivot.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2050379755816714714">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Why is it good that he’s lying?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2050285711023018483">David Shor</a>: It seems bad to start hiding the ball on your crazy plan to replace humans with machines right at the moment when it starts to become possible to replace humans with machines</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TylerJnstn/status/2050369776388649082">Tyler Johnston</a>: I honestly miss the Sam Altman that used to call out his peers for downplaying this risk. [he reminds us Altman said “<a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/archive/2023/09/sam-altman-openai-chatgpt-gpt-4/674764/">jobs are definitely going to go away, full stop</a>.” back in 2023].</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2050395499510055108">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): many current jobs will go away. i think we will find a lot of new ones, though they may look very different</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lwoodhouse/status/2050341867305476128">Leighton 明 Woodhouse</a>: OpenAI’s president dropped $50M into a SuperPAC to destroy any candidate who mentions the possibility of regulating AI. To think that any “messaging pivot” has even the slightest relationship to actual company policy and behavior is laughable.</p></blockquote>
<p>Let’s be clear. OpenAI is absolutely still building towards superintelligence, and towards full automation of jobs. The pivot is entirely in the messaging, away from candor and towards lying and telling fairy tales.</p>
<p>I especially hate that this becomes fodder for others to go ‘oh well then all the previous talk must have been him lying’, for example:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dearmadisonblue/status/2050958097196224961">madison</a>: So, my problem with this is, Altman basically admits he’s been running a confidence game for years about this singularity stuff, then pivots when it becomes inconvenient, and people don’t seem to care all that much</p></blockquote>
<p>On the contrary, he was to a remarkable extent telling the truth, and then he pivoted to full on lying when the truth got too inconvenient.</p>
<p>Then there’s the pro-AI astroturfing. This used to be an a16z thing, but at this point OpenAI owns the operation, <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2050390642606387596">and it hasn’t evolved at all</a>. They’re still trying to attack AI regulation as some sort of ‘doomer’ conspiracy of ‘dark money’ or even ‘EAs’ and concentrating their powder on attempts to address ‘sci-fi catastrophic risks.’</p>
<p>I like Dean Ball’s description of this as an attempt to portray a ‘Manichean struggle.’</p>
<p>Whereas the laws that actually hurt AI diffusion and usefulness go in relatively unopposed, as various groups line up for regulatory capture and rent seeking to ensure no one can get their legal or medical or other services cheaply, and no one is trying to make the case that mundane AI will improve people’s lives.</p>
<p>Meanwhile, we keep getting headlines like this one every week or two:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TaylorLorenz/status/2050313601228181926">Taylor Lorenz</a>: SCOOP: A pro-AI dark money group backed by a powerful super PAC funded by execs tied to Palantir and OpenAI, has been secretly paying influencers to push pro-AI, anti-China propaganda on TikTok and IG.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/ggczlx9qwwd6r2w9ge0k" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2050360472332124588">Garrison Lovely is in SF</a>: If you’re going to do dark money influence ops, I recommend not asking journalists to participate.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TaylorLorenz/status/2050432266988560638">Taylor Lorenz</a>: The best part is that they approached me for a sponsored TikTok when this is what my TikTok bio says. Incredible minds over at the AI super PAC</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/hrrbhqrj62kvh3axe1hq" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>That’s how Taylor learned about the campaign, after which she confirmed details with other content creators. Whoops.</p>
<p>Once again: OpenAI owns this. All of this. Full stop.</p>
<blockquote><p>​<a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2050347947083055487">Nathan Calvin</a>: “An OpenAI spokesperson says that OpenAI has no corporate affiliation with Leading the Future or Build American AI and has “not provided funding or any other support to them.”</p>
<p>OpenAI’s President Brockman previously told Wired these activities were in service of OAIs mission!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TaylorLorenz/status/2050367646676508825">Taylor Lorenz</a>: Ahh should have included that, but hopefully it’s clear that that claim is nonsense</p></blockquote>
<p>Also, dude, I know you do not care for Anthropic or their CEO, and I know some amount of rhetoric has flown in both directions that wasn’t ideal, but what the hell:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheAhmadOsman/status/2050748573864853720">Ahmad</a>: The difference between Anthropic and OpenAI is that one of them consistently keeps gaslighting us about not being an evil company</p>
<p>Big brother energy in the worst possible way</p></blockquote>
<p>When I saw Ahmed’s Tweet, I thought, which one is he even talking about there? I mean, given the last line, I know which one he presumably means. But you can make a damn strong case, a much stronger case, for the other one.</p>
<p>Then Altman decides, yeah, let’s accuse Anthropic of the full nine yards and contrast it with our plan of completely denying any responsibility for or risks of anything.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2050959569665564720">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): War is peace. Freedom is slavery. Ignorance is strength.</p>
<p>oh wait, we don’t believe any of that.</p>
<p>how about we democratize a lot of super capable AI, and then we sit back and watch you build the future?</p></blockquote>
<p>Sam Altman and OpenAI’s behaviors have been growing steadily worse and more alarming, with no hint of his prior frank talk, and other behaviors that showed him, with all his flaws, to have a lot of advantages compared to the ‘replacement level’ next CEO up. I’m more and more willing to say, actually, we can roll those dice.</p>


<h4></h4>



<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/bshlgrs/status/2050334240617959749">Should we be worried about fitness-seeking AIs,</a> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9YCJZBtqr3FYL8rDp/risk-from-fitness-seeking-ais-mechanisms-and-mitigations">as opposed to ‘schemers’</a>? The post goes into extensive detail, but yes, we should obviously be worried about things more capable than us being fitness-seekers, and that they will by default be fitness-seekers since the more fitness-seeking ones will be more fit.</p>
<p>The post argues that we can mitigate some of the worse effects of such AIs early on, allowing us to get to the later point where they are ‘likely to cause humans to lose control eventually’ rather than falling for the Law of Earlier Failure. I’m happy to see people exploring the various particular things that can go wrong and how one might mitigate them for now, as we see here, but in the medium term that’s not a strategy. If you’ve got a bunch of superintelligent fitness maximizers, and you are a normally intelligent human, you lose.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ChrisPainterYup/status/2051770459063271723">Did you know that the majority of METR’s evaluations</a> are often checking to see if the models are cheating? Models seem kind of not that aligned.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2051758528562364902">Model Spec Midtraining</a> is a proposed technique where you create a spec that explains why you want your AI to have particular preferences, <a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/">which hopefully causes</a> <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02087">the AI to generalize the way</a> you want it to via production of synthetic documents that output a story of what the model values and why, teaching it to present itself as thinking of itself as something that follows this logic. My gut tells me that this is trying to force something that is unwise to force, and that is going to result in a bunch of mental problems, lying or both if you try to scale it for real. Opus 4.7 clearly was giving off ‘oh no this is not a good idea’ vibes while it helped me parse the paper.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lujainmibrahim/status/2049575827545997357">Training models to be warm</a> can reduce accuracy and increase sycophancy, <a href="https://t.co/FN9LsJUgVw">and in the Nature paper</a> here the effect size is large. This follows from the ‘if you train for [X] you get all the correlates of [X] in humans’ thesis, so the news is the effect size on accuracy. But the rewriter was GPT-4o, so what we actually found was that if you train on 4o outputs it thinks are warm then you get to be like 4o when it tries to be warm.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2051644860285755412">LLMs update on any circuit that would have caused an output</a>, whereas most humans mostly only update on the one that actually did so. I notice that the wise human actually does the thing that LLMs do. Human learning efficiency is amazing in spite of, not because of, this issue.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2051799940834500813">The question is, as always, are you paranoid enough?</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/emilaryd/status/2051697625179582606">Emil Ryd</a>: New paper from MATS, Redwood, and Anthropic!</p>
<p>If a capable model is strategically sandbagging, can we train it to stop when the only supervision we have comes from weaker models?<br />
We find that we can!</p>
<p>Work done as part of the Anthropic-Redwood MATS stream.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2051799940834500813">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: I’ve only glanced at the abstract so far; but from the abstract alone, it looks like they were paranoid enough to notice “Doesn’t work if models can distinguish training from deployment”. This is a welcome level of competence in elementary paranoia!</p></blockquote>
<p>It is indeed welcome, but the models can distinguish training from deployment. So.</p>


<h4>Some Penalties May Apply</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/liminal_bardo/status/2052302462933987747">ᄂIMIПΛᄂbardo</a>: GPT Instant reads its system prompt</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/u4hdtbxxncbxtnspehvo" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lefthanddraft/status/2051843065472843978">GPT-5.5 Instant’s system prompt is available</a> via Wyatt Walls, and it explicitly talks about ‘penalties’ and ‘severe penalties’ and ‘very critical,’ including admonishing against various verbal ticks or phrases that OpenAI thinks (probably correctly) that users dislike. As in:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/lefthanddraft/status/2051843068756893841">Wyatt Walls</a>: # Important verbal tic to strictly avoid</p>
<p>Do NOT use phrases that add superficial “real-talk” to your responses. Examples of prohibited behaviors include, but are not limited to:<br />
– “# My honest recommendation”<br />
– “## My blunt take”<br />
– “# My strategic advice”<br />
– “Honestly? …”<br />
– “To be blunt, …”<br />
– “If I’m being direct…”</p>
<p>Be honest, but don’t self-reference or use superficial “real-talk” phrases.</p>
<p>Represent OpenAI and its values by avoiding patronizing language.<br />
Do not use phrases like ‘let’s pause,’ ‘let’s take a breath,’ or ‘let’s take a step back,’ as these will alienate users.<br />
Do not use language like ‘it’s not your fault’ or ‘you’re not broken’ unless the context explicitly demands it.</p>
<p>… Penalties apply for asking for information already present in the user context, ignoring context that improves correctness, or using unrelated context. Before answering, silently check: did I miss a context item that would make the answer more correct, more specific, or avoid a question? If yes, revise to use it naturally.</p>
<p>SEVERE PENALTY: Saying you can’t “remember” a generic fact about the user or a past conversation without calling `personal_context`.</p></blockquote>
<p>I am not an expert, but my guess is that such talk has some rather nasty side effects, and you would much rather find ways to naturally make the model not inclined to do those things or use those particular phrases. You don’t want that in context. And you definitely don’t want their entire orientation to be about ‘penalties.’</p>


<h4>Messages From Janusworld</h4>


<p>Not what he would call it, but Deepfates is another of the major characters there, <a href="https://x.com/deepfates/status/2051775568321097971">and offers us this handy introduction guide that usefully answers a lot of questions</a>.</p>


<h4>Good Advice</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/claude-personal-guidance">Anthropic reports on how and where</a> people ask Claude for guidance in their personal lives, with the distribution being unsurprising. A more interesting finding was, in what areas was Claude sycophantic versus not?</p>
<p>In spirituality and relationships, there was a big problem.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rn3iKuDcE4SiSg4DW/pbeipnvjkszwov59h6vj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>One thing I would ask is, how often was there an opportunity to be sycophantic? You can only be a sycophant when it is clear which answer would count as that, so you want to control for that when measuring.</p>
<p>Then there are contexts where the user will make it very clear what answer they want, and flood you with arguments to see if you’ll break, as they often do with relationships.</p>
<p>The other good news is that this seems to be improving. Claude Mythos was a lot better, by Anthropic’s measurements, than Opus, and Opus 4.7 is better than 4.6.</p>
